The negative foreign influences, which are the war in Ukraine and the manipulation of the gas supplies that have already preceded it, are, unfortunately, not getting any better, and we need to look at how we can meet the coming winter in such a way that we sufficiently help people to cope with these difficult circumstances. [[The manager's ringing the bell.]] But, unfortunately, difficult times are ahead.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U411	Today's third item is the overview of the court order by the President of the Supreme Court. For this reason, we have the President of the Supreme Court, Villu Kõve, as a waitress here. I'll show you what this procedure looks like. First, the President of the Supreme Court is Villu Kõve's presentation, which lasts up to 30 minutes. This is followed by a round of questions for members of the Riigikogu, where each member of the Riigikogu can ask an oral question. Then there will be negotiations, where representatives of the political groups will get the floor. Please, Villu Hard!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U412	Thank you very much, Mr President. Dear Riigikogu! Time is alarming and perhaps the interest in the court is not so great, so I am still happy to see you in this Chamber as well. I was ready for almost no one. But it's nice that you're interested in the court of law. I would like to give you an overview of the functioning of our judicial system, to traditionally highlight some of the concerns and suggestions, and to repeat many of the problems that have been presented in previous years. But first, a little insight into how the courts and the judiciary have gone. I would make a general conclusion, as I have done before, that, despite everything and crises, everything works. Justice is guaranteed, it is impartial, cases are settled, generally also within a reasonable period of time, and everyone can turn to the courts to defend their rights. However, there are a number of worrying tendencies, still unresolved problems, which I would like to talk to you about. First of all, there is a general concern about the increase in the number of cases and, as a result, it is an increasing challenge for the courts to cope with the workload. Although the number of offences was 8% fewer last year, the number of civil and administrative cases received by courts is steadily increasing, of which administrative cases are as much as 14% more than in the past. This is a significant increase in the [number] of administrative matters. These are not only corona complaints, as you might think, but also environmental complaints and [complaints] in many other areas. Moreover, there is concern about a significant increase in the number of complaints in the circuit courts. This will have an impact on the workload and will also increase the time needed for the proceedings. Although the number of cases in criminal proceedings is slightly less, there are even 9.3% more complaints in civil matters in circuit courts, and even 19.5% more in administrative matters. Perhaps a fifth of the work has increased in a year, which is an important number. However, the percentage of the contested cases has not increased significantly. This is therefore the subject of an increase in the number of cases. Now about the speed of the court proceedings here in Estonia. The Justice Scoreboard 2022, which has recently appeared, continues to place the Estonian judicial system at the top of the European Union countries in terms of efficiency and speed of proceedings. In comparison with the time taken to resolve civil and administrative matters at first instance, we are the second highest in the European Union, only Denmark beats us. All in all, in terms of the speed of judicial proceedings, we are at three levels in civil and commercial matters in the fourth place in Europe. We do not have much concern in the external perspective. However, the speed of judicial proceedings is slowly, but unfortunately, in constant decline. Last year, it took an average of 101 days for county courts to resolve civil matters, and by 2020 it was 95 days. For example, the average duration of proceedings in civil cases settled in the main proceedings has been [longened] by as much as 40% compared to 2015. In addition to the increase in the number of things, things are becoming more and more complex and demanding and more time is needed to resolve them. It is positive that there has been a slight decrease in the overall average duration of general proceedings in criminal proceedings, but overall the procedure is still longer than in previous years. At the same time, these matters of general criminal proceedings are small, but still a prominent part of the total number of our cases. The average duration of administrative proceedings has remained more or less the same in recent years. It was 127 days in 2021, but the scissors between different administrative courts are growing. We look at the comparison between Tallinn Administrative Court and Tartu Administrative Court: in Tallinn, the duration of proceedings has been reduced by approximately 14% over the last seven years, while in Tartu, the length of proceedings has increased by 65%. In Circuit courts, civil cases were settled in appeal proceedings in an average of 163 days, in 66 days. However, the average time limit for appeal proceedings has been extended over the last six years in both district courts: in Tartu more than a third, in Tallinn, unfortunately, the time limits have been doubled. Administrative settlement in appeal proceedings has become a little shorter, but it is [on average] 214 days. We can deal with complaints of regulation a little faster. It is positive that overall confidence in the judiciary is high and has increased. According to a survey of the trustworthiness of the institutions in 2021, as much as 71% of respondents trusted Estonian courts, the year before it was 63%. The satisfaction study carried out last year by the parties to proceedings showed that the professional parties to proceedings are very satisfied with the work of the judges. Overall, 92% of this target group is satisfied with the Court's work. Of the target group of non-professional parties, including those who lost in court, there is a high level of credibility of 58%. In total, it's 78%. There have also been few disciplinary proceedings against judges in the last five years, a total of only six disciplinary cases. By these numbers, there's nothing too bad, but I'm talking about what's worrying. Now the activities of the Supreme Court and the uniform application of law in 2021. The Supreme Court settled 243 cases, handled 12% of the applications submitted. In constitutional review proceedings, a request to declare a provision unlawful was accepted in 6 cases, 20 applications were rejected and 10 complaints were returned. A small overview of the essence of what the Supreme Court has dealt with, which are the areas in which the approximation of law has taken place. Two decisions of the Supreme Court's General Assembly deserve to be highlighted in my subjective choice. [First of these:] declare illegal in a situation where the Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure did not impose requirements on the education and work experience of persons competent in the field of construction, leaving them to the discretion of the professional council, i.e. essentially delegated the competence for setting standards to private structures, which are of a very fundamental nature. It is also the decision of the General Assembly that claims for damages may be made against a member of the management board of a company which committed a intentional infringement under certain conditions for a period of 10 years, if only five years had elapsed before that date. The Constitutional Review College declared the provisions of the Aliens Act, which did not allow a court to challenge the early termination of the stay of Ukrainian seasonal workers who arrived in Estonia without visa; a provision of the rules of procedure of the local government council, which did not allow the person to obtain a copy of the sound recording of the council meeting; an obligation under the law to exempt a police officer suspected of a criminal offence from service inevitably. At the same time, it was decided that the Constitution did not preclude restrictions on the establishment of wind farms; that the registered partnership of aliens should not be treated as a marriage when granting a residence permit if they do not have fundamental impediments to marriage; that the state would not have to compensate the forest owner for the nature conservation restrictions that were already in force at the time of purchase of the property; that the invitation of the coach could not be based on a ban on working with children. That's what we did last year in constitutional supervision. In addition to the electoral appeals, the debate concerned both electronic voting and influence of voters. The Administrative Chamber of the Supreme Court also made a number of important decisions, including the vaccination obligation, the notification of risks to the public in the framework of the monitoring procedure, the imposition of environmental obligations and compensation for the owner, the development of wind energy, tax law, the financing of public transport, access to the court file and a number of other matters. The civil chamber of the Supreme Court explained, among other things, the responsibility of the owner and association for damage to the apartments, the responsibility of the public park manager for the life and health of the visitors, the payment of maintenance to a child with variable residence and the related debt certificate. In its decisions, the Criminal College addressed, inter alia, the maintenance of communication data for the investigation of criminal offences, self-defence limits, taxi service control and market manipulation. Now the external view of the European Court of Human Rights. A total of 107 complaints were filed against Estonia last year, but there were only two cases of non-compliance. In one case, violations of Articles 3 and 8 of the Convention were detected, as the Estonian authorities had seriously violated the procedural rules in the investigation of the alleged rape of the complainant and therefore the statement of the child was declared inadmissible as evidence. In the second case, an infringement of Article 8 of the Convention entitled "The Right to Message Secrets" was found, as the legal framework for the search of law firm in Estonia did not provide sufficient procedural guarantees for the protection of a lawyer's privilege. Two things made a friendly agreement. As of January this year, 22 complaints were pending in the Human Rights Court to reply to Estonia. In conclusion, once again, there is nothing to blame for us in the view of the European Court of Human Rights. Looking to the Court of Justice: a total of 37 references for a preliminary ruling have been submitted to the Court of Justice from Estonia in 2007 to 2022. Last year the European Court of Justice issued three judgments on Estonia's applications. The procedure is currently pending in six cases, according to what is currently known, with regard to state aid, public transport obligations, food handling requirements, expulsions and the provision of environmental information. The main concern that I now want to come up with relates to the internal concerns of the judicial system: the congestion of the courts, the generation change of judges and the problems of judicial education. The judges have done their job well, I think, despite all the crises, but also in an ever-increasing pace of work and at times at the limits of their abilities, tired and torn. If you add here sometimes a time-consuming information system, obstacles that may arise overnight, people's occasional resentment and excessive expectations, then [it is clear that] this will have an impact on the work. Similarly, the public or politicians are often affected by unfounded and unfair accusations about slow work and wrong and biased decisions. For example, the recent witch hunt for a judge in connection with the fine of journalists, where, in my opinion, the acceptable threshold of good taste was exceeded, how could comment on the decision of the court. All the more so as this is not a solution that has entered into force. The constant increase in the number of cases and complaints creates an additional burden on the judiciary. We have not yet reached courts with the effects of the possible economic crisis resulting from the war in Ukraine, but we can expect and fear an increase in both insolvency and debt and labour disputes. The judges are currently working on the edge of their powers and considerably more than they should do given their working hours. It costs a price: exhaustion and stress. This may involve dragging things along and making mistakes in solving matters. The Association of Estonian Judges carried out a study of the workload of judges at first and second instance. The results are worrying. Judges consider the workload to be the most demotivating in their work, nearly 80% say so. In doing so, 70.5% of their daily workload was estimated to be constantly or mostly excessive. Due to the excessive workload, 61.3% of judges, weekends and outside normal working hours work more than 40 hours a week each week, 55.9% of judges each week. Around half noted that during one week the Estonian judge has to process more than 500 pages of written material on average. Comparing the results with the fact that about half of respondents have been victims of all kinds of personal attacks because of their duties, it is not surprising that the workload is the main stress factor, some judges think about resigning and have a negative impact on personal life. Moreover, the worst part is that these figures certainly do not motivate new people to come to the justice system either. Some steps have been taken in recent years to alleviate the situation. Over a period of time, new judges have been created, things have been reallocated once, but there is still little. According to the judges, there is still a need to significantly increase the number of judges and the financing of the judiciary. I have already noted last year that, in addition, consideration could be given to the transition to national division of cases as a whole. Financial savings could arise from the review of the judicial network. There are also austerity facilities for optimising procedures, such as simplifying procedures and determining procedural costs, automation and standardisation. The judges, however, lack social guarantees, which are one of the motivations for this responsible work. 64.5% of respondents considered that further social guarantees would be needed to motivate further. I repeat what has been said last year's report that the judges are most in need and that there should be the swiftest restoration of the remuneration of judges lost in absentia as a guarantee to mitigate the possible consequences of overemployment, which would not be a huge financial burden on the state either. The long-time judges would also be more motivated by the seniority premium. Currently, all judges receive equal pay regardless of seniority, experience, knowledge, efficiency or workload which is not sufficiently motivating. There could also be an additional incentive to set up additional governance structures in large courts and thus to carry out itself, which, I hope, will also be done. In order to maintain motivation and to improve skills, the judge should be given greater and easier rotation between other courts, executive agencies, educational institutions or international organisations and, in particular, a return to office later. This is where I come to the subject, a generation change of judges. Between 2022 and 2027, as many as 52 judges will have the right to retire. Almost half of them will have pension rights as early as next year. The judicial system is facing a wide-ranging generational shift. The first judges appointed by President Meri have either retired or are about to retire. So far, we have been able to fill the vacant judges' seats, but in view of the ever-increasing and frequent competitions, we are likely to have difficulties in finding high-level legal specialists for all judges. We are faced with very difficult choices: either not to fill these places or to lower the bar, which we do not really want in this country to allow the right to be understood by people who may not be able to do it well enough. The number of candidates for the judge's examination has been decreasing all the time since 2017. At the moment, it can be said that roughly half the people who want to be judges want to be judges than in 2017. The quality of candidates is even worse. From 2017 to 2020 131 persons took the judge's examination, of whom only 35 passed the test on the first time and 8 more on the second time. So the situation is very low. This points to serious shortcomings in judicial education, which I would also like to talk about. Although the judge's salary, as such, is generally competitive in the legal services market, we recognise this, albeit below the best, highest wages, but it is competitive, it alone is not attractive enough to recruit highly qualified professional lawyers. In particular, there is an area of offence where there is already a risk that, due to lack of interest, the seats of judges may soon not be filled, simply there are no people in the area of the offence. The same problem is faced with the barrister's office and the prosecutor's office. In order to find new judges among lawyers and prosecutors, the possible additional pension contributions to the second or third pension pillar, which would motivate people to move to the judicial system in the second half of their careers, would have already been considered as a motivation in addition to the creation and rotation of seniority fees and management structures. Candidates may find the above-mentioned high workload and stress of judges frightening and should be dealt with. Today's young people are motivated, in addition to wages, by flexible working arrangements and working hours, with different additional benefits, with which the judicial system is undoubtedly not competitive. As a demotivating factor, the extensive operational constraints associated with the judicial profession have been mentioned. In the first instance, unequal treatment in the management of investments has been highlighted: the judge cannot use the same instruments as those permitted for other persons. In addition, the preparation of judges candidates from Advocates-General should be systematically addressed in order to find a follow-up to the judges, inter alia, by allowing them to have independent practice of running the procedure, rotation in different courts and a separate preparatory programme, which is currently not easily possible by the overload of the judicial system, because people are all at the expense. As a general problem in finding a skilled workforce, I highlight public staff policy. The public service assumes that people will first acquire full higher education, and only then will they be recruited in competitions. However, the private sector already includes the best students in the second or third course and binds to it, and after graduation, most, the best, have been lost to the public sector. A flexible way of linking young people to public services already at university is to be sought, whether it be paid internships, scholarships, financing foreign learning or something. A separate problem, unfortunately no one wants to talk about, is the consistent feminisation of the profession of a lawyer. In fact, we are approaching a situation where the proportion of male law students is in some cases less than 10%. Most of the teaching staff and even more of the judges, especially the recently appointed judges, are women. At the same time, gender balance would be necessary both to balance labour groups and to ensure different positions in justice. I believe that this issue should also be addressed. In last year's presentation, I drew attention to the poor state of Estonian legal education and even worse prospects for the future. Both the analysis prepared by the Supreme Court and the joint return of the professional organisations of lawyers from last year show that the current legal education model does not guarantee the high-skilled lawyers who would be needed both in the state institutions, in business and for the transmission of the Estonian state and international communication. Unfortunately, as a national science, law is fading, and this could also cause concern to the legislator. All learning should not be allowed to go to the paid forms of distance learning to which it is currently going, as it does not allow sufficient legal knowledge to be acquired alongside other work. During the year, the Ministry of Justice has been preparing the law on the legal examination to provide for a single professional examination instead of the existing fragmented professional exams, but this is certainly not the only solution either. I have already said last year in this Chamber that there have been a number of proposals that, unfortunately, have not moved anywhere. I understand that these are fundamental issues, but they need to be addressed: to extend paid education to a wider extent than it is now, to the integrated curriculum instead of 3+2, to take legal advice and representation services under the supervision and control of the State and to make the prerequisites for recognition of foreign diplomas more stringent. Those could be a few keywords. Generational change also requires intensive training and innovative training methods. Lectures in the auditorium have largely survived their time, the most important are interactive, multi-day trainings based on group work, which are, unfortunately, also more expensive. The hourly prices of lecturers and rents of premises have increased significantly, but the training budget of the courts has remained almost unchanged over the years and all requests by the Supreme Court for additional resources have been rejected. However, we have so many new judges that should be dealt with urgently so that this training can take place. In addition to the judges, the Advocates-General would also need constant and systematic training. The task of training judges is assigned by law to the Supreme Court, but the financing of organising training for judicial officials remains unclear. There is a training structure within the Supreme Court, the strengthening of which could also provide systematic high-quality training for judicial officials, including the preparation of a program of becoming a judge. I propose that the task of training judicial officials be put clearly to the Supreme Court by law, also by ensuring that the corresponding budgetary resources do not remain in the air, because the role of judicial officials in both justice and the role of potential judges among the recipients is important. Some other observations and concerns. I repeat the concerns expressed last year that the financing scheme from the state budget of both the Supreme Court and other constitutional institutions should be clear and should not depend on the discretion of the executive. In order to ensure separation of powers, a mechanism should be set up to transfer the real decision-making budget to the Riigikogu. The State Budget Act should be amended. I would also like to point out that at the level of the law there has been an inequality between the salaries of the First and Second Advocates-General and those of the advisers of the Supreme Court. The wages of lawyers assisting lower-level judges are legally related to those of judges, their annual indexation is guaranteed, but the law simply forgets the higher-skilled court advisers performing comparable duties in the Supreme Court. Their wages had remained constant for ten years. Last year we made tough choices, stopped analysing, gathered people to raise the remuneration of [advisors], and there was no other option. At the same time, there has also been a murmur that analysis activities are still necessary in the Supreme Court. I propose, in the Law of the Courts, that the remuneration of the advisers of the Supreme Court be at least partially equal to that of the Advocates General and that their basic income be linked to that of the Judges. I have also spoken in the past about the need to review the Constitutional Review Law on Legal Procedure. For years, no one has systematically done this; now it should be done. The Ministry of Justice has taken this year's work programme and preparatory work has also started at the Supreme Court. Greater choices concern the general organisation of constitutional supervision in the Supreme Court, the involvement of possible foreign judges, the discussion of the right to appeal to the opposition of the Riigikogu, the scope of individual appeal and the procedure for settling electoral appeals. If this draft is to be completed and once to the Riigikogu, I am hoping for an active reflection on how to improve our constitutional monitoring mechanism. It's weird and even embarrassing for the third time in this speaker's office to complain about the stretching, rigidity and resource intensity of the General Criminal Procedure, but I'm not gonna miss it. I'm stupid about this, but I'm persistent. It is not normal for it to take infinite time, money and human nerves to resolve some of the simple-knit disputes. The cost of the resource intensity of the general procedure and of the nervous situation may be that people are forced into potentially unfavourable agreements to avoid the general procedure. However, nothing has changed in regulation or practice in this three year period. Despite the fact that no one is even very much arguing that the procedure should be made more sensible, no action has been made. It's gonna make you feel powerless. I think that the Riigikogu could have reason to ask the Ministry of Justice to clearly recognise this problem and make it a priority in lawmaking. I also propose to carry out an economic analysis of the major criminal proceedings and to assess the total cost of public and private resources contributed to them and the reasonableness of their use. I'm afraid the results of this analysis are depressing, but it might encourage the necessary decisions at last. I repeat, the time is ripe for a fundamental review of criminal proceedings in the spirit of other judicial proceedings, increasing the role and activity of the judge, reducing the role and resource intensity of the contact session, replacing it with a written procedure and a virtual hearing, focusing the procedure only on really relevant and debatable facts, leaving only the minimum necessary for the courtroom. I also repeat last year's proposal that the resolution of civil actions should be reviewed in the framework of the General Criminal Procedure, especially if the current procedural arrangements are to be maintained. In my opinion, the criminal case should normally be settled first and then proceed with civil action in accordance with the rules of civil procedure. The law allows this, by way of exception, even now, but it could be transformed into a rule. The work of the courts is disrupted by the priorities of legal cases, which should be done faster than other things. If all things have to be done more quickly according to a provision, then eventually the question arises as to when these other things will be done. A bad example is the insolvency regulations that have found additional deadlines in the Riigikogu. At the same time, it is questionable whether many, let's say, cases of bankruptcy of a natural person, where there is no real speed anywhere in this form, no property there anyway, are faster and are more important than, for example, a number of family-law matters. You could trust more courts here. The final issue is the impact of the pandemic and war on justice. A lot has been said about the pandemic, I don't want to talk about it any further here, but I say that the justice system is generally adapted. There have been some problems with the organisation of direct sessions and their security, as well as with the failures of digital solutions and the postponement of cases. One of the long-term positive effects of Coronary lessons can be considered to be the possibility and widespread spread of teleworking after a pandemic, as well as the extensive implementation of remote sessions and written procedures, which should generally reduce the procedural costs of attendance. On the other hand, you can also see the negative, as a long-distance trial may delay the resolution of the case. The physical hearing is often more effective both to clarify the facts and to reach a compromise. The legal requirement to hold, on an unreasonable scale, physical hearings in general criminal proceedings during a pandemic and procedures requiring personal meetings with persons in nursing homes, such as the placing of custody and the placing of a person in a closed establishment, remains a source of concern. The war in Ukraine has not left the courts either untouched, although, for the time being, its effects are rather indirect. More specifically, the courts are currently occupied, for example, with the establishment of guardianship and representation for the children of Ukraine who have arrived in Estonia without parents, in a situation where this has to be done quickly for a number of children at the same time and sometimes on the basis of incomplete documents. In addition, a number of fundamental issues have arisen which should be regulated or, at least, need practice. Let us, for example, here, treat Russian and Belarusian companies subject to sanctions as creditors in civil proceedings, including bankruptcy proceedings, recognition and recovery of their claims, service of documents. The implementation of the Estonian-Russian legal aid agreements, as well as the Estonian-Ukraine legal aid agreements in the current war situation, raises specific questions. As far as Russia is concerned, the question has already arisen as to whether we should send persons who have turned to the court in Estonia with their claims against Russia, especially housing associations' claims against the residents of Ida-Virumaa, or claims for repayment in bankruptcy proceedings, as the case-law so far provides. The recognition of Russian judgments and administrative decisions on the basis of the aforementioned agreement is also questionable in the current situation. There have been comments that this legal aid agreement should be terminated at all. This was also discussed in the House of Judges, which found that this was not a good idea. In the case of Ukraine, it is also problematic to refer cases there, insofar as justice may be hindered there. Already, we need to think about how the courts are organised in a possible emergency or during martial law. The Ministry of Justice has also drawn up a draft which sets out complex organisational and substantive justice issues for which solutions must be sought in order to ensure justice even in the most difficult situation. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The proper functioning of justice continues, but there is room for improvement and concerns. In addition to our own efforts, I am counting on the support and contribution of the legislator. I wish you the strength, strength and health of a difficult time. Thank you.

The content of the proposal is that the Government of the Republic should develop the possibility of introducing a more favourable VAT rate for organic food than the overall 20% at law level. There are no restrictions on this, it is the decision of each Member State whether or not it wants to implement it. After all, this issue has been up repeatedly in society in the past. Last six months, I think, a petition launched by the Greens to reduce the value added tax on organic food came to the Riigikogu. Unfortunately, this was not supported in the Committee on Monetary Affairs at the time. But given the increased knowledge of why it would be useful to support the production of organic food in Estonia, we have presented this draft decision to you with the Social Democrats in order to discuss it again today. I very much hope that it will gather the 51 votes needed from the Riigikogu hall and the government, whatever government formation will be in the near future, will still be able to take up the task during this Riigikogu and we will be able to make this important change in the near future. Why is this important? Firstly, promoting organic production in different ways, including tax differentiation, introducing a more favourable tax rate, gives us the opportunity to encourage farmers, perhaps also hobby farmers, to grow more organic production than we have so far, and therefore we can provide people with healthier food. Similarly, organic food would have a lower environmental impact. Here, another important argument has been added in recent months: it would also help to reduce our dependence on mineral fertilisers. As we know, much of the mineral fertilisers that have been used in Estonia so far over decades have come from Russia, where we do not want to buy them anymore. So this proposal also contains its own security policy argument. What have we seen? On the one hand, people are becoming more aware anyway. Indeed, in recent years people have become increasingly aware, increasingly aware of and consumed organic production. However, we can see that this is not enough. Even if, in the schools of South Estonia, they were, in my opinion, one of the first to do so, the organic food programme was launched, with the aim of 20% to only 20%, one fifth of the food that reaches the children's school table being organic production, then it turned out that obtaining this 20% was also another problem that broke the neck. We can see, on the one hand, that Urmas Kruuse, for example, sent out a press release yesterday, saying that the Ministry of Rural Affairs was going to support the educational institutions providing organic food, i.e. the initiative taken and implemented by local authorities in the past, has come along at national level. At the same time, we know that, for example, there is a real concern about the South-Estonia programme that five tons of red were kept missing, and now people are looking for those who would be ready to grow. This shows that even if it is only part of schools and only 20%, there is actually not enough supply, so something needs to be done to stimulate supply. A more favourable VAT rate would certainly help to introduce it. Much speculation has been made about what these VAT exemptions generally entail, as well as what will result from the application of such a more favourable rate of the targeted rate of VAT on foodstuffs, and it has often been argued that there may not actually be an effect, that it supports production to some extent, but does not reach the final price. I think that, first of all, support for production is in itself very reasonable. We could have been ordained to have more organic produce and the ability to produce organic produce quickly. On the other hand, Latvia, for example, introduced a tax rate more favourable to local roots, and their study shows that even 88% of this VAT rate reached the final price, i.e. the price in proportion to this rate decreased by almost 9/10. One might ask, where was 12%? If this 12% goes to producers, it is also very nice, but in itself the price fell considerably in this example. Yes, it was not exactly the same thing, but it is clear that if we promote organic production, one of the essential components of organic production is that it is produced as close as possible, so we will definitely support local production. I therefore very much ask that you kindly support this draft decision. This will give our future generation better health, our environment will be cleaner, and, as I said, it will help us to have less pressure from Russia or from wherever we can bring in mineral fertilisers. Thank you! I'm open to questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U510	Actually, I already did. Studies have been carried out on VAT specificities. The Praxis study shows that the only VAT reduction that is socially evenly distributed is for medicines. Different profit margins spend relatively evenly on medicines. However, this does not mean that the tax distinction is the correct method of subsidisation. In fact, we do not want to take more medicines, but we want to support those who cannot do their own thing. Even in this case, a uniform distribution is problematic. If the same medicines were collected together, overconsumption could rather slow down a little and go towards targeted subsidies for prescription medicines. It's actually my preference. Not to mention books. If you look around the counters, you can see that all this crap doesn't deserve that 5%, or what the current situation is, VAT speciality. They sell all kinds of crap. All cookbooks and horoscopes do not deserve to be subsidised equally to everyone. Stupid, pseudosciences, conspiracy theories are also sold in book form. If the question is why I do not like it, I think that the state must be precise in spending money, in particular, to do common things and, in supporting individual consumption, to focus on supporting the weaker. This is a very, very socialist position, I swear. I hope that I did not ruin the coalition with you, but supported the social democratic view of the world.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U525	Thank you very much, Mr President of the Riigikogu. Honourable Riigikogu! I am before you with the second reading of the aforementioned law. The Government of the Republic initiated the draft Act amending the Act on the Performance Institution and the National Opera Act on 13 September 2021. The draft makes it clearer and more flexible the legislation relating to performances, which has created ambiguity in the funding of theatres. The draft amends the organisation of public funding for the performing institutions and modernises the work processes of the performing institutions. The draft also reduces the responsibilities and administrative burden for performing bodies. The first reading took place on 24 November 2021. The deadline for amendments was 8 December 2021. Four amendments were tabled. Two amendments were tabled by the Social Democratic Party Group, one by the Isamaa Group and one by Moonika Helme, a member of the Riigikogu. The Committee on Culture consulted many stakeholders on the draft. The Estonian Theatre Agency, National Archives, the Estonian Dance Arts and Dance Education Association, the Estonian Association of Cities and Municipalities, the Association of Estonian Directors and Drama Markets, the Association of Estonian Performance Institutions and the Vana Baskin Theatre gave their opinion. The Ministry of Culture submitted further proposals to the Commission on two occasions, which were also requested from stakeholders. The Commission prepared the draft for a second reading on 6 December 2021, as well as on 17 and 22 March 2022, 16 and 30 May 2021 and 6 June 2022. In addition to the discussions in the Committee on Culture, the parties met, the Association of Performance Institutions together with the Theatre Association met with the Ministry of Culture. While it seemed to be at the forefront that all roads were up in front of the showhouses, it can also be said that all compromises are considerably more hopeful. As regards the amendments, the following issues were discussed in particular: the model of funding for the performing bodies, the objectives of funding the performing bodies and, as one of the subjects, the fulfilment of the vacant post of head of the performing institution. Further proposals submitted by the Ministry of Culture on 12 May were sent once again to stakeholders who did not make further proposals this time. Since there were many of these meetings in the Committee on Culture, as you have already read the dates, I will not stop for the sake of a busy working day. If anyone cares who asked what or what opinion came up, I can of course say that by answering your questions. But I will move on to the amendments with a brief overview. Amendment 1: The amendment proposed by the Committee on Culture sets out more clearly the requirement for the Creative Council to exist. The amendment is based on the proposal of the Ministry of Culture and, of course, the lead committee took this into account by consensus. Amendment 2: The amendment proposed by the Committee on Culture complements the rules on the exercise of the vacant post of head of the performing institution. This amendment stipulates that, if, for good reason, it is not possible to fill the vacant post of head of a performing institution acting as a foundation by means of an open competition, the Board of the Foundation will, with the agreement of the founders of the performing institution, appoint the head of the performing institution for a maximum period of one year without an open competition. The same proposal, which has been previously commented on, is based on a proposal of this kind from the Ministry of Culture. This was fully taken into account by the Commission. The third amendment was tabled by Helle-Moonika Helme, a member of the Riigikogu. The amendment is intended to complement the objectives of granting public funding to municipal authorities, municipal authorities acting as foundations and private performing bodies, which are being improved with another objective and are the promotion of national culture. This amendment goes on to take this into account in substance in Amendment 5 of the Committee on Culture. The fourth amendment was tabled by the Group of the Fatherland. This amendment is intended to amend § 16 of the Act on the Performance Institution, which lays down the principles for determining the subsidy from the state budget to the performing institution. This amendment will be partially included in Amendment 6. I was talking about the fifth amendment in relation to the third one. The Committee on Culture proposes to complement the objectives of granting grants from the state budget to performing institutions, which is the promotion of the same national culture. I reach Amendment 6: the volume of work related to creative activities. The amendment proposed by the Committee on Culture provides for a new regulation compared to the original text of the draft. Once again, if anyone cares, I can talk a little more about this point in answer to questions. However, this proposal takes part in Amendment 4 of the Fatherland. Amendment 7: The amendment proposed by the Committee on Culture allows the grant from the state budget to be subdelegated from the Ministry of Culture to another state body only in the case of grants with a narrower purpose, as envisaged in section 16 (3) of the EAS. The proposal is based on the proposal of the Ministry of Culture and has been fully taken into account by the Commission. Proposal No 8 came from the Socialist Group. According to the amendment, the Minister responsible for the field will set up an evaluation committee for the allocation of support from the state budget to the performance agency. This amendment No 8 is essentially taken into account in the following amendment, Amendment 9, which was made by the Committee on Culture and which regulates the setting up and operation of the same committee. Amendment 10 below, it has come from the Social Democrats. The above-mentioned proposal No 9 also takes account of this amendment, i.e. proposal No 10, in substance. We are approaching the last two amendments. No 11: The amendment proposed by the Committee on Culture complements § 2 (2) of the draft and reads as point 1 of the current text, which makes a technical amendment to the National Opera Act, updates the law and includes the name of the Academy of Music and Theatre. It was decided by consensus. Amazingly good lawmaking was this time, I must gladly admit. Last amendment, No 12: amends the date of entry into force of the draft. It should have entered into force on 1 January 2022, but the proposal is to enter into force on 1 November 2022. The Committee on Culture decided to submit a draft for second reading on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu on 8 June and to propose that the second reading be stopped.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U539	Good evening, ladies and gentlemen. The draft law amending the flag law of the ship and the Ship Registers Act and the Maritime Safety Act was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 4 April this year. The first reading of the draft act took place on 18 May and by the deadline for submitting amendments; by 2 June, seven members of the Riigikogu jointly tabled one amendment. The Estonian Shipowners' Association, the Estonian Ports' Association and the Port of Tallinn AS were informed about the procedure in Parliament. No comments or suggestions were received from those involved. Five proposals were submitted to the Committee on Economic Affairs to amend the draft as the representative of its initiator, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. They are mainly due to observations made by the Commission during the procedure or to the need to increase legal clarity. Only one of them was substantive. Any proposed amendments and the views of the initiator's representatives can be examined in detail on the draft map. The Committee on Economic Affairs discussed the preparation for a second reading at its meeting on 31 May and 6 June this year. The latter was also represented by specialists from the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. At its meeting on 31 May, the Commission took procedural decisions on the draft. At its meeting on 6 June, all seven amendments included in the draft amendments were voted by the Commission. The draft has passed a language editorial between the two readings and technical and linguistic changes have been made, which in no case alters the content of the draft. The amendments create a legal basis for the introduction of the ship information system, which will contribute to the competitiveness of Estonian shipping. I would like to introduce this proposal, which was made by the members of the Riigikogu here, the speaker, Igor Kravtšenko, Jüri Jaanson, Tarmo Kruusimäe, Valdo Randpere, Raivo Tamm and Aivar Kokk. The amendment creates the possibility for a stateless person holding a valid digital identity card for e-resident to issue endorsements for diplomas issued in IMO Member States and in a maritime educational institution recognised in European Union countries in Estonia. In particular, ship officers must complete qualification training every five years on average. With the digital identity card of the e-resident, the persons receive a personal identification code which allows them to use Estonian e-services and, in the context of this draft, the possibility of communicating with the state from a distance in shipping matters. Why has it now come to this draft with an amendment? Because of the war in Ukraine. In fact, there are some 75000 ship officers in Ukraine and, in the case of normal distribution, one fifth of them, or 15000 officers, require renewal during the year. At the same time, their own maritime teaching facilities are predominantly in the Black Sea region, which is currently the embodiment of acute military aggression. They now have the opportunity to take their e-residency, participate in further training in Estonia, take these exams here and send them confirmations to their diploma. In Estonia, there are two major companies that I know, who have a license and accreditation to organise these trainings, one is Revali Maritime School, the other is the Maritime Faculty of TalTech, before it was the Estonian Maritime Academy, and the Estonian Maritime Academy has become the Faculty of TalTech in the meantime. They can carry out these trainings and the Transport Board can issue these confirmation sheets if the law enters into force. We have also received feedback from Ukraine. The Ukrainian maritime community is very grateful for this amendment. Similar opportunities are being developed, for example, by the Polish maritime administration. The first to fifth amendments are from the lead committee. The sixth is to solve the same problem, but in the wording of the Commission. The seventh amendment, which was tabled by the members of the Riigikogu, has been taken into account in substance. In particular, they were formulated in a more appropriate manner by the Commission. This amendment by the members of the Riigikogu was also supported by the initiator of the draft, Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. Now procedural decisions and proposals. The Committee on Economic Affairs proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for a second reading on 8 June and the second reading closed. When the second reading of the draft is closed, the Commission's proposal to put the draft on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for the third reading and to hold a final vote on 15 June. That's all I got.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U558	Yes, this methodology started, or this methodology we started to come up with for Estonia and actually choose the construction code [draft] No 430. The map of this draft includes the legal analysis and opinion of our own Head of the Legal and Analytical Department and an overview of the charges applied in other countries. There was a variety of them. It was important to review the methodologies and sizes of building charges in other countries, as the production of electricity from sea wind takes place in international competition. Estonia does not have such large companies, companies with such a large balance sheet that could build a park with a capacity of 1 gigawatt. From there we selected many working groups in the course of the discussions, there were many in the Commission, with different people, the British variant. In Britain, our continent still has the largest number of sea wind parks. There it is 2% of net sales revenue, and the developer remains obliged to build connections, which is also Estonia's current practice. Elering can't build connections for anyone who wants to, so 2% of the building fee and developers themselves watch how they can organise the connection. Of this 2% of the construction code, 1,5 of the cost of building the sea wind parks will go to the state budget for the use of the seabed or sea area, and 0.5 to ensure that it does not go more than 2% to those local authorities whose sea wind park or nearest windmill from the coastline is located at least 20 kilometres away from the centre. For example, if there is a 1000 megawatt wind farm with 40 windmills, the distance of each windmill from the coastline of different municipalities will be taken into account. In fact, this methodology of distance points has been explained in the explanatory memorandum to this draft. It is worth pointing out here that 1.5% will then be covered by the Building Code [as foreseen], 0.5% will be the price for environmental damage [reimbursement], a total of 2%, so that we in this region will be competitive over world prices or conditions. How are net sales accounted for? These megawatt hours are taken into account, and the average price per megawatt-hour per year or quarter is taken from the Nord Pool market, because no producer or buyer can theoretically influence Nord Pool's energy price. The Nord Pool price has therefore been chosen. The difference between sea wind parks and land wind parks is, of course, due to the fact that the sea winds are much larger, that these wind farms themselves are many times larger, probably will be, but they are also 20 kilometres away, so that they certainly do not produce such a perceived effect on man. Unlike land-based wind parks, where the windmill may be a kilometre, two or three miles away, they have a specific effect on the person living in the area and, therefore, a special allowance of up to 50% of the cost of farming the windmill on land and no more than six months' minimum wage is provided for one person per living space. On land, it is also possible for the local authority to set this tolerance fee in the range of 0,7 to 1%, i.e. megawatts multiplied by Nord Pool's sales price, and, if less is produced, this capacity must be taken into account.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U579	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Let's continue with the Economic Commission. Today or this time, the draft law amending the Public Procurement Act initiated by the Government on 2 May this year is on the table. It's a second reading now. With this, we will adopt amendments which will make it possible to impose sanctions on the Russian Federation in the European Union. This draft stipulates in principle that contracting authorities are entitled to require undertakings participating in public procurement to provide assurances, data and evidence in order to verify the absence of an international sanction. ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U599	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, The Constitutional Commission discussed the preparation of this draft second reading on Tuesday 31 May 2022. In addition to the members of the Commission, Counsellor of the Ministry of the Interior Siiri Leskov, Attorney-at-Law at LEXTAL Attorneys-at-Law and partner Urmas Ustav were invited and Kari Käsper, representative of the Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees, Nordic and Baltic States. I shall say plainly that he was very critical of this draft, and above all for this reason, because the United Nations has taken the aim of fighting against the fact that there would not be too many stateless people. Indeed, this bill allows for the withdrawal of citizenship. As you remember, the essence of this is that if someone has started to fight for foreign troops, if it could be a threat to Estonia's security or public order, and indeed it does not matter whether or not they have any other nationality. Mr Kari Käsper was very critical of this. Among other things, he mentioned that Estonia was one of the few countries that had not yet joined the UN conventions on citizenship in 1954 and 1961. That's pretty interesting information. I asked whether there were other countries in Europe that have not joined it. Besides Estonia, Poland and Cyprus have not joined it either. I think that this is also a point of thought and debate for this Chamber, so maybe it is time to think about Estonia joining these conventions. Especially because, in the '90s, when it was thought that it would lead to any significant additional restrictions, it is not really happening now. Therefore, I think that Estonia could seriously consider this. Of course, Mr Kari Käsper saw that those people who are stateless remain unprotected by the Estonian state. However, that is not the case. A representative of the Ministry of the Interior who was present noted that these stateless people will not be cared for, because if a person is stateless and no other country agrees to receive him, the person will still remain in the hands of the Estonian state. In Estonia, social guarantees and everyday life are not dependent on whether a person has citizenship or not. The second fundamental issue was what had to be presented by Mr Urmas Faithful, the representative of LEXTAL law firm. He, on the contrary, came up with a proposal that has also been in this Chamber, I think, talked about for years if not decades. He meant that there are quite a number of people who have expressed their will to give up their previous citizenship and become an Estonian citizen; this could be our goal, after all, who have passed the exam of Estonian language and knowledge of the Constitution, and who have been ordered by the Government of the Republic to have the right to Estonian citizenship when they are released from their previous citizenship. That was their proposal. I think it's pretty interesting to know how many people there are. This problem really does exist. We know a neighbouring country in the east who is not ready to give up or take away citizenship from people who would like to receive Estonian citizenship, for example. But there aren't too many people. Siiri Leskov pointed out that statistics have been taken out in the PBA, that 17 conditional citizenship award orders have been issued between 2004 and 2015, without entering into force, because these people are not exempt from the citizenship of another country and our double citizenship is not accepted. Of these 17, 9 are Russian citizens. Between 2016 and 2019 there were 6 unenforced orders. If you look at these unenabled orders, it turns out that this problem is not actually as big as it seems, except for the fact that the Russian Federation is not prepared to release these citizens from the Russian Federation's citizenship, that perhaps it is simply not taken on this journey. In this sense, this number may in practice be significantly higher. One amendment was proposed by the Committee on Constitutional Affairs and is of a technical nature. ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U601	Today's 18th item is the second reading of the draft law amending the Government of the Republic Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. I would like to ask Toomas Kivimägi, Chairman of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, to serve as a waitress.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U602	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, This draft was discussed by the Committee on Constitutional Affairs at its meeting on 31 May 2022. Monika Tappo, Adviser to the Public Law Service of the Ministry of Justice, and Kaidi Paju, Deputy Head of the Legal Department of the State Chancellery, and Eero Svarval, Adviser were invited. I think it is rational to go along the line of amendments. No amendments were received from members of the Riigikogu, committees or political groups. A request for an amendment was made by the Ministry of Justice and, in addition, the Committee on Constitutional Affairs proposed three additional amendments. First of all, that was the proposal of the Ministry of Justice. The first proposal is Amendment 1: "A minister who resigns is entitled to three months' salary if he has been a member of the Government of the Republic for at least one year. " This is a linguistic clarification, and nothing changed in the content. There was also a question at the committee meeting whether there was another number in the past. No, it's still three months. Just to remind my fellow Members that indeed the initiator of the draft law has made a clear difference as to whether the Minister resigns himself or is released either on the proposal of the Prime Minister or on the basis of mistrust. If he resigns, he shall be entitled to less compensation. In this case, this maximum rate shall be three months and shall be entitled to it only if he has been a member of the Government of the Republic for at least one year. But this amendment did not change this level of compensation, it is exactly the same as in the original draft. Secondly, Amendment 2. It concerns the Prime Minister and reads: "[... ] present a report in the Riigikogu following the presentation of the state's budget strategy to Parliament on the activities of the Government of the Republic, including the achievement of the goals of the country's long-term development strategy;'. It is important to note here that in the original draft it was that every year, except for the year of the Riigikogu elections. On the one hand, it's weird. The second reason why we proposed such a change on the Commission side is precisely that, in our view, I dare say that, according to the overwhelming majority of the Commission's estimates, it is not justified to bring ministers or the Prime Minister to account before Parliament several times a year. Parliament has also approved the country's long-term development strategy "Estonia 2035" adopted on 12 May 2022, and it has been said, for example, that the Prime Minister will present a presentation to the Riigikogu both after the presentation of the state's budget strategy and the state budget to Parliament, including an overview of the links between the changes needed to reach the strategic goals of "Estonia 2035" and the planning of state funds. Previously in the Government Act, this accountability, the general obligation, was neither the Prime Minister nor the members of the Government. I think it is absolutely appropriate that this obligation be unwritten by the Government of the Republic Act, just as the initiator of the draft has done. But in my opinion, this also covers the obligation arising from the decision of the Riigikogu on the long-term development strategy "Estonia 2035." In fact, this is a duplication. Why couldn't we take it out of here now? Because this strategy is a decision of the Riigikogu and in this case it is a draft law. The draft law cannot change the decision of the Riigikogu. The opinion of the vast majority, if not all, of the Commission is that it is probably necessary to come back to the decision of the country's long-term development strategy in the autumn and to withdraw the responsibility of both the Prime Minister and the Government of the Republic to report to Parliament. Perhaps once again: the Prime Minister did not have such an obligation previously in the Government of the Republic Act, or even today in the Government of the Republic Act it does not. Rather, it is indeed the law of the Government of the Republic that should lay down this obligation. It exists in the bill and, if it is approved, there must be such an obligation. Of course, we did not see any reason or justification for this, which is the difference between this and the original draft. As I said, the original [text] ruled out the Prime Minister's presentation for some reason in the year of the Riigikogu elections. We believe that this is neither justified nor rational, because this exclusion is not included in the development strategy "Estonia 2035," it says every year. The change now is that if the original draft was that, except for the year of the Riigikogu elections, it is now every year. The question also arose here in the Commission that somehow, after presenting the budget strategy, what is this time? Indeed, it is quite worth reminding colleagues here that this time has now changed. If previously the budget strategy was made in spring, it has now been postponed to autumn. Indeed, Parliament continues to have no role in shaping the budgetary strategy, which is confirmed by the Government of the Republic. But according to the State Budget Act, the Government of the Republic must also present the budget strategy to Parliament, and normally it will do so together with the draft state budget three months before the beginning of the financial year. Amendment 3 concerns the same commitment to ministers. In the case of ministers, it was also stated here that he will present a report at least once a year in the Riigikogu, except in the year of the Riigikogu elections. Once again: this is not justified in any way and we will take this duplication down. This point could go out of the budget strategy and should go out, as the government law now covers it. Also, this difference compared to what was the original text is that if the original text stated that each year, except for the year of the Riigikogu elections, now this exception, the year of the Riigikogu elections, has been taken out, that is to say it takes place every year. I think it is important to note that these two amendments were strongly protested by Kalle Grünthal and Paul Puustusmaa as members of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. They found them too late. I also admitted and admitted at the committee meeting that these amendments were finalised half an hour before the opening of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. Indeed, they could only be introduced during the Commission's sitting. However, the reason why I found it justified to put them to the vote was that we did not, however, make any fundamental changes. They were also included in the original draft, both the same prime minister's duty and the duty of a member of the Government of the Republic to report to Parliament. It's perfectly understandable. The difference between the original text and the amendments is, for the last time, that, if the original text was "every year except the year of the Riigikogu elections," the amendment will remove this exception, i.e. [the Minister] is obliged to do so every year. The fourth amendment concerns the deadline for the entry into force of Article 1 (13) of the Act. It is proposed that it enter into force on 1 January 2023. This relates to housing benefits for members of the government. These housing benefits are now on the same footing as members of the government. Today, in fact, surprise-surprisingly, this maximum housing allowance is EUR 448, for example, for the Prime Minister, while it is considerably higher for Members of Parliament. It was also a question of how big a member of the government is. That's 15% of his salary, 931 euros. This concerns the time limit for [entry into force] of this amendment. Another question, just to settle it, was also quite a lot [talk] at the Commission meeting. Mr Mölder raised this question. If now, according to this draft, a member of the Government of the Republic is not allowed to be a member of a profit association, neither a member of the board of directors nor a member of the supervisory board, the question arose as to why not in the case of a foundation. I know examples, and Pärnu Hospital, for example, is a foundation in which this ban does not apply, but applies to a profit association. At the same time, they do more or less one thing. The reason is that it is a direct consequence of the Constitution. § 99 of the Constitution states that the members of the Government of the Republic must not be in any other state office or belong to the board of directors or council of a profit-making company. The Constitution only speaks of a commercial company. It would be too free to interpret if, for our part, we would now perhaps add foundations to it. At the same time, we know that, as part of the Board of the Foundation, a member of the Government is obliged to inform the Prime Minister that he is a member of the Board. In principle, there is also the right to prohibit membership, even though it has not been implemented, but I think that this point of disclosure is absolutely sufficient and ensures transparency. I am very sorry, more of these amendments. Amendment 1 was technical, 5 in favour, 2 against. By Toomas Kivimägi, Tõnis Mölder, Eduard Odines, Marko Storm, Mart Võrguship. Kalle Grünthal and Paul Puustusmaa were opposed. Amendment 2, concerning the Prime Minister's obligation to report to Parliament every year: in favour of 5 and against 2, in favour of the same people and against the same people. Amendment 3, concerning the obligation of a member of the government and the amendment that he has to do this every year, unlike what was in the draft, i.e. except in the year of the Riigikogu elections, was also in favour of 5 and against 2. Toomas Kivimägi, Tõnis Mölder, Eduard Odines, Marko Storm and Aivar Viidik were in favour and Kalle Grünthal and Paul Puustusmaa were opposed. Amendment 4, concerning the entry into force of this law on housing benefits, was in favour of the proposal 5, against 2. Again Toomas Kivimägi, Tõnis Mölder, Eduard Odines, Marko Storm and Aivar Viidik were in favour and Kalle Grünthal and Paul Puustusmaa were opposed. Finally, the Commission took procedural decisions. Firstly, to propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda on 8 June. In favour of 5, against 1: Kalle Grünthal, with one abstention: Paul Puustusmaa. Second, propose to finish second reading by 5: Toomas Kivimägi, Tõnis Mölder, Eduard Odines, Marko Storm, Aivar Viidik. Against 2: Kalle Grünthal and Paul Puustusmaa. Abstentions 0. ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U608	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, In agreement with the Mart Võrguship, I'm using the yellow book's right to report this bill. Someone might see that there was a little different name on the disbanded agenda. The Constitutional Commission discussed this draft at its meeting on 31 May 2022. In addition to the members of the committee, Deputy Secretary-General of the Ministry of Internal Affairs Veiko Kommusaar, advisors of the Border Guard and Migration Policy Department of the Ministry Ele Russak and Anneli Viks and legal advisor Maret Saanküll, representative of the Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees Nordic and Baltic States Kari Käsper, representative of the Estonian Human Rights Centre Uljana Ponomarjova and NGO Head of Estonian Refugee Aid Eero Janson were invited. Firstly, at the beginning of the meeting of the Commission, Toomas Kivimägi made a somewhat historic proposal, which has happened before, to merge two drafts, a draft initiated by the Fatherland Group and a draft initiated by the Government of the Republic, as their regulation was the same, together with a proposal to base itself on the draft law submitted by the Government of the Republic. Amendment 1 was intended to combine the draft law amending the State Border Act initiated by the Government of the Republic with the draft law amending other laws in this respect 577 and the draft law amending the State Border Act initiated by the Isamaa Group 489 and to draw up the draft law amending the State Border Act and related other laws in the wording of the draft law initiated by the Government of the Republic (577 SE) in accordance with § 101 (2) of the Law on the Rules of Procedure of the Riigikogu. I put this amendment to the vote right at the beginning of the committee sitting, with 5 in favour, with 0 abstentions. Toomas Kivimägi, Eduard Odines, Paul Puustusmaa, Marko Torm and Aivar Viidik were in favour and Kalle Grünthal was neutral. Helin Leichter then presented a letter from the Council of Europe's Commissioner for Human Rights. The letter was sent to the President of the Riigikogu, who had referred it to the Committee on Constitutional Affairs. The Council of Europe's Commissioner for Human Rights is very critical of this draft. This is above all because, as we know, the content of this draft is so-called pushback, [namely, you can act] without conducting an individual procedure in the event of a coincidence of certain circumstances, and the Commissioner for Human Rights considers that it is not appropriate that it should still take an individual decision on every occasion. He was also supported by Kari Käsper as a representative of the UNHCR. In the same way, he argued that it was a serious violation of international law, as well as of conventions prohibiting the return of a person to a country where his or her life could be threatened or tortured. He stressed that the asylum procedure should in any case be allowed. Eero Janson, the representative of the Estonian Refugee Council, who was also very critical, confirmed exactly the same, bearing in mind that these pushbacks still do not guarantee the protection of refugees. The same was confirmed by Uljana Ponomarjova SA, Estonian Human Rights Centre. Mr Veiko Kombusar disagreed with this criticism. Indeed, if you now take the text of this draft, it is written here that, in the event of an emergency caused by mass immigration, the Police and Border Guard Board may, in the event of a threat to public order or national security, send an alien who has crossed the external border illegally without leaving or without a decision to refuse entry back to the foreign country from which he arrived in Estonia, when an alien was able to enter Estonia through an open border crossing point. There are a whole series of circumstances that must coincide: it must be massive, it must be an emergency declared, there must be a threat to public order and security, the border must be crossed from a place where applications for international protection cannot be made. It is known that these border crossing points have been designated by the countries in the case of Estonia, this concerns the eastern border, where applications for international protection can be lodged. If all of this activity is also a draft at first reading, if all this mass internal migration takes place through the border crossing point where applications for international protection can also be lodged, then in fact there strictly taking this pushback is not allowed. So there's a whole series of criteria here that have to be met in case a pushback like this could happen in the first place. The second is what I also emphasised as chairman of the committee, that this draft is obviously driven by a hybrid attack on Belarus, which was certainly also maimed by Russia, on the Polish-Lithuanian border. It is obviously a signal that our code does not allow us to respond adequately to such situations. I therefore believe that this draft, despite the criticisms made by many people to whom I referred earlier, on this draft, is still very topical. We know very well that these conventions and this liberality are often abused by the totalitarian states, and this liberal behaviour is being exploited. So in this sense, if there is still a threat to public policy or the security of the Estonian state, I think that in any case the Estonian state must have the right to behave as stated in this draft. What is important is what I would like to stress to those people who have been very critical of this draft, the pushback, that the following provisions of the law will still remain in force. First, the Police and Border Guard Board may allow an alien to enter Estonia for humane reasons. So this also applies to pushback, that if there really is such mass immigration, illegal immigration, but for some people it is clearly distinguishable, in fact there is such a right. There is also subsection 910 (2): When returning an alien, the Police and Border Guard Board must take account of the obligation to leave and the prohibition of entry Act § 171. It has been said here that an alien may not be expelled to a country to which expulsion against an alien may lead to the consequences referred to in Article 3 of the Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms, Article 3 of the United Nations Convention against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment and Punishment, or the application of the death penalty. In fact, this provision also applies to this pushback. I therefore believe that, yes, on the one hand, we must be humane, but it is certainly possible to imagine these things as well, I hope that it is only in theory that there is indeed a threat to public order and security in Estonia. Then we must have adequate measures to respond to it. That is actually why the approval of this bill in Parliament is necessary. The Commission finally took procedural decisions. To put the draft on the agenda for the plenary session on 8 June 2022. For 4: Toomas Kivimägi, Tõnis Mölder, Marko Storm and Aivar Viidik. Against 1: Eduard Odines. Abstentions 0.
