Hillar Petersen of the Foundation Family Foundation gave us an overview of his views and suggestions. In short, he and his representative organization were very positive about this draft. He saw a number of important things in it, I would briefly mention them. The first was that all children's allowance would be equalized, i.e. both the first and second children's allowance would be raised to the same level as the third or more children, i.e. EUR 100. The second positive shift that he pointed out is the rise in support for families with children, i.e. if three to six children grow in a family, this support will rise from EUR 300 to EUR 700 this year, and if children grow more than a month, it will rise from EUR 400 to EUR 900 today. What was specifically pointed out by him, and what I personally believe, and in which the Commission's discussions have also resulted in a solution, is that really such a huge and fundamental change, in addition to the change in numbers, is precisely about indexing support for families with children. This is a completely different approach to child benefits and support for a family rich in children than it is today. And another is that we are really applying this system, that if a child has either reached the age of 16 and completed his or her studies or continues to study until he or she reaches the age of 19, then the family will also be able to receive a child-rich family allowance for him or her, and later, if he or she gets out of this age limit or finishes his or her studies, then the income of a family with children's wealth will be reduced by one third. So we've made this system considerably smoother. It was an inclusive by such a brief review. We then went to the subject of the presentation of the amendments. And I would really like to draw attention to what were the Commission's amendments, which the Commission also supported. I am not going to read all these other fine amendments one by one here, but it is important that the Commission's proposal is to raise child support from 60 to 100 euros. The second proposal was to increase the support of single parents from EUR 19 and from 18 cents to EUR 40. It is just in the interests of the large Chamber that it is necessary to clarify that, if, at first, the Commission intended to treat it as one particular amendment, the Commission decided in the course of its procedure to raise this amendment into a separate proposal. In fact, it was clear that the cross-party support received a significantly higher amount than was the wish of the initiators of the draft. The third proposal concerned a number of people who are expected to receive child-rich families for a longer period than today's legislation provides for. In other words, we have raised the multiple in the system of child-rich families and set an age limit for them that is not related to learning, i.e. until the age of 21. The idea really is that otherwise children grow up in child-rich families in such a way that their age differences are different and then this [support] reduction occurs proportionally until the youngest child becomes adult. In the case of multiples, such as triplets, this leap must not be such that the entire family loses much in the income of child-rich families, as multiples become 16 at the same time or complete their studies, becoming 19 years old. We gave them a buffer until we were 21. This is a completely new approach compared to the regulation in force today. The fourth and fifth proposals concern support for a family rich in children. As I said, the first proposal is to raise today's EUR 300 to EUR 700 for families of 3 to 6 children and to move from EUR 400 to EUR 900 for families of children with more children. The sixth proposal is to reduce the allowance for a family rich in children by one third at the age of 16 and at the end of their studies or, if the child continues to study until the age of 19, to stop paying the allowance at the end of the academic year. So once again, this is a proportional reduction, which I think is a very clear and strong advantage compared to today's system. If today's child becomes an adult or completes his or her studies, the family automatically loses the support of a family rich in children. At the same time, we know that after the child's birthday, the family's income does not increase in any way and the expenses do not fall automatically. Obviously, it's going to be a while before a child gets his or her life and maybe this family doesn't have to be supported in that form anymore. And what has actually been one of the great wishes of the initiators of the draft: it is not, once again, specifically, not child-related support, but support for the family, for the family, for the family, for the purpose of [helping] the Estonian population to [increase], who has really helped move towards having three or more children born into families. We have agreed on this border in society, we say that a child-rich family has been around since the third child. It is valid in today's system and we really did not want to change that. And I think it is an important understanding that if child support is primarily intended for a child, then support for a family rich in children is intended specifically for a family rich in children. Now the amendment, which is also important and which is worth marking. It is a pity that there are very few lines in this room, because I think this is a very important point. ParlaMint-EE_2022-06-08_U708	Thank you. First of all, as far as the support of a single parent is concerned, we understand in this sense in the same way that if we want to compare something, we can still compare comparable numbers and comparable subsidies. Now, the other thing about multiple issues. As I understand it, we still deal with it in the same way that if three children grow up in a family, two are multiples, and one is either younger or older, then it is that the benefit of a family rich in children is paid to that family when three children are born. If one of these children loses the right to benefit under the law [for three children], well, you gave a tragic example of what is unfortunately happening in life, children also go to the other, if the family loses the right to benefit from a family rich in children, then the amount will be reduced by one third. If the multiples live on [nicely], we did not really apply this system of support that is in force today until the child receives 16 and finishes his or her studies or until he or she reaches the age of 19 when he or she goes on to study, but we set this ceiling for 21 years. It is precisely with the idea that, if there are several children of different ages, the [end of payment of the allowance] will proceed smoothly by step: one third, one third and one third. In the case of multiples, the situation clearly arises [various]. Then let's give an example of triplets: in the case of triplets, when children all turn 19 and graduate from high school, the family automatically loses the support of a family rich in children from that day. Our proposal and wish was for this transition to be smoother. That's why we made this, let's say, the maximum buffer of one and a half years, right in the case of multiples, so it would be smoother and better. In fact, the families raising multiples just said it would give them a better sense of security.

Firstly, due to the haste, no in-depth analysis has been carried out on the draft, but given the unequal treatment of children, which is the result of this draft, the draft could be a violation of the Constitution and the principle of equal treatment. Secondly, no account has been taken of the impact of the increase in subsidies on the maintenance aid to be paid. If the parents are separated, only half of the planned increase in benefits will be received by the parent raising the children, as 50% of the increase will go to cover the parent's maintenance obligations. This means that the amount of maintenance to be paid is reduced by the increase in family benefits. This is particularly unfair for families rich in children. By doing so, we put parents raising a child alone in an even worse position and significantly reduce the responsibility of the other parent. Unfortunately, those who suffer from it are mainly mothers. Thirdly, the same multiplicity that I asked about and, in fact, the questions remained. If a 21-year-old family member works or moves away from the country, for example, does the country still pay the family allowance for him because he is a multiple? Or the same sad example: if one of the multiples dies or is still born, will the second multiple be entitled to the same benefit for the age of 21? Fourthly, the operative part of the draft law, which has also been referred to from the outset by the Social Insurance Board. IT solutions that need to be made to index child-rich family allowances and to take account of differences are not easy to do in this time frame. Fifthly, with the draft coming into force, it is unclear to whom § 21 (4) will apply, I mean this gradual decline in a family rich in children, two thirds and one third. Is it only for those who cease to benefit a family rich in children from 1 June 2023, or should it extend to all families who have previously been granted a family rich in children? These were just a few of the nuances that I noted when reading the draft and which I was unable to ask about in the committee on Monday, because at one o'clock I had to leave the Commission and before that, the Commission's proposals were put to the vote without discussion and without the possibility of asking questions. In absolute poverty, 6.7% of single-parent families, 2.7% of single-parent families, 2% of families with three or more children and 1.2% of single-child families live in absolute poverty.
